Tests for Independence of High-Dimensional Nonstationary Time Series
Yunyi Zhang

Abstract
This manuscript studies the problem of independence testing between two high-dimensional time series without assuming weak stationarity, allowing their autocovariances to vary over time. To this end, we propose a bimodal weighted-average test statistic that removes the bias induced by temporal dependence under the null hypothesis, thereby avoiding the need to whiten the time series prior to hypothesis testing—a procedure that is challenging in high-dimensional and nonstationary settings. To facilitate statistical inference, we develop a dependent wild bootstrap procedure. On the theoretical side, we derive a concentration inequality for quadratic forms of time series data stemming from a class of high-dimensional, nonlinear, and nonstationary processes. This result enables us to derive the asymptotic null distribution of the proposed test statistic and to establish the validity of the bootstrap algorithm. Numerical results show that the proposed test attains desired size and good power performance even when the dimension exceeds the sample size or when the data-generating process exhibits time-varying autocovariances. In contrast, tests based on whitening time series fail to maintain correct size in the presence of unstable autocovariance structures. Since nonstationary autocovariances commonly arise in real-life time series data, our work offers a robust procedure for independence testing.



Keywords:
Independence Test; Nonstationary time series; High-dimensional data; Quadratic form; Wild bootstrap.

1
Introduction

1.1
Overview

Suppose that we observe a mean-zero, vector-valued time series
\mathbf{z}_{t}=\left(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}\right)^{\top}\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}+d_{2}}
for
t=1,2,\cdots,T.
We consider testing the null hypothesis
H_{0}
that
the sequences
\left\{\mathbf{x}_{t}\right\}_{t=1,\cdots,T}
and
\left\{\mathbf{y}_{t}\right\}_{t=1,\cdots,T}
are independent, against the alternative hypothesis
H_{1}
that they are dependent.

Independence testing in time series has attracted considerable attention in economics, financial data analysis, machine learning, and biological studies, as evidenced by the growing body of literature, including
Kawaller et al. 1987
,
Shao 2009
,
Besserve et al. 2013
,
Huang et al. 2016
,
Thurman et al. 2019
,
Wang et al. 2021
,
Günther et al. 2022
,
Yuan & Shou 2024
,
Ghoshal & Shao 2026
. Several reasons motivate this line of research. For example,
Robbins & Fisher 2015
leveraged such testing procedures to investigate the relationship between inflation and currency exchange rates. In addition, when a time series comprises many features, practitioners may assess dependence structures among subsets of variables, where independence suggests separation into distinct communities. Furthermore, independence testing aids model simplification
Wang et al. 2021
. If two time series are found to be independent, they may be modeled separately rather than jointly, leading to a substantial reduction in model complexity.

Early contributions, such as
Koch & Yang 1986
,
Hong 1996
,
Brock et al. 1996
,
Kulperger & Lockhart 1998
,
Duchesne & Roy 2003
,
Shao 2009
, primarily considered independence testing for scalar processes, while subsequent studies
El Himdi & Roy 1997
,
Bouhaddioui & Roy 2006
,
Robbins & Fisher 2015
,
Wang et al. 2021
,
Meintanis et al. 2022
,
Chu 2023
generalized the hypothesis testing problem to multivariate and functional time series.
A common approach in the literature — see, for example,
Shao 2009
,
Robbins & Fisher 2015
— assumes that
\mathbf{x}_{t}
and
\mathbf{y}_{t}
follow autoregressive models with independent innovations and reduces the hypothesis testing problem to testing independence between fitted innovations. Although effective in low-dimensional settings, such an approach faces two challenges in high dimensions or when autocovariance stationarity is violated. First, in high-dimensional settings, consistent estimation of vector autoregressive models typically requires structural assumptions such as sparsity or low rank in the coefficient matrices
Han et al. 2015
,
Basu & Michailidis 2015
,
Krampe et al. 2021
,
Zhang 2023
,
Wang et al. 2022
, which may be restrictive in practice. Second, when the autocovariance structure is nonstationary, fitting autoregressive models no longer recovers the underlying innovations, thus invalidating distributional approximations. Moreover, assessing autocovariance stationarity can be difficult in high dimensions
Zhang & Chan 2025
.

To address these challenges, this manuscript proposes a bimodal weighted-average test statistic to test independence between two high-dimensional time series. The proposed method allows the dimensions
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
to exceed the sample size
T,
imposes no stationarity assumptions on the autocovariance structure, and does not rely on vector autoregressive modeling. By avoiding these restrictions, the procedure is broadly applicable and robust to model misspecification.

The remainder of this manuscript is organized as follows: Section
1.2
reviews related literature. Section
2
introduces the test statistic and a dependent wild bootstrap algorithm that assists statistical inference via computer simulations. Section
3
establishes the asymptotic distribution of the test statistic under
H_{0},
and proves the validity of the proposed bootstrap algorithm. Sections
4
and
5
respectively demonstrate the finite-sample performance and the applications of the test through numerical experiments and real data examples. Additional results, including further numerical experiments and detailed proofs of the theoretical results, are postponed to the online supplement.

1.2
Related literature

This section provides a review of the literature on independence testing and its practical applications; further review of methods for analyzing high-dimensional vector autoregressive (VAR) processes is deferred to the online supplement.

Independence testing.
Research on testing the independence of two stationary scalar time series dates back to
Haugh 1976
,
Pierce 1977
, with more recent studies including
Dalla et al. 2022
,
Giraitis et al. 2024
,
Leong 2025
. This line of research has been extended to vector time series; see, for example,
Bouhaddioui & Roy 2006
,
Hallin & Saidi 2007
,
Robbins & Fisher 2015
,
Wang et al. 2021
,
Dette & Kroll 2024
. We also refer to
Meintanis et al. 2022
,
Zhu et al. 2024
for independence testing in the functional data setting, and to
Chen & Liu 2018
,
Yao et al. 2018
,
Zhu et al. 2020
,
Albert et al. 2022
,
Cai et al. 2024
for independence testing with high-dimensional independent observations. Despite this extensive body of literature, to the best of our knowledge, relatively few studies have been conducted on independence testing for high-dimensional nonstationary time series, where the temporal dependence can affect the null distribution of the test statistic under
H_{0}
, potentially leading to size inflation.

Applications.
Independence testing serves as a key component in many complex algorithms. For example, as shown in
Hoyer et al. 2008
,
Spirtes et al. 2012
,
Peters et al. 2013
, it complements causal discovery methods by determining the direction of causality. In addition, the works of
Song et al. 2007
,
Song et al. 2012
employ independence testing for feature selection. We also refer readers to
Tsonis et al. 2015
,
Cliff et al. 2021
,
Cliff et al. 2023
,
Yuan & Shou 2024
,
Doubovikov & Aksenov 2025
, among others, for several real-world data applications.

1.3
Frequently-used notations

This manuscript uses the standard order notations
O(\cdot),o(\cdot),O_{p}(\cdot),o_{p}(\cdot):
For two numerical sequences
a_{t},b_{t},t=1,2,\cdots,
the notation
a_{t}=O(b_{t})
indicates that there exists a constant
C>0
such that
|a_{t}|\leq C|b_{t}|
for any
t\in\mathbf{Z};
and
a_{t}=o(b_{t})
indicates
\lim_{t\to\infty}\left(\frac{a_{t}}{b_{t}}\right)=0.
In particular, we say
a_{t}\asymp b_{t}
if the conditions
a_{t}=O(b_{t})
and
b_{t}=O(a_{t})
hold true simultaneously. For two random variable sequences
X_{t},Y_{t},
we say
X_{t}=O_{p}(Y_{t})
if for any given
0<\varepsilon<1,
there exists a constant
C_{\varepsilon}>0
(depending only on the value of
\varepsilon
) such that
\mathbf{pr}\left(|X_{t}|\leq C_{\varepsilon}|Y_{t}|\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon
for any
t;
and
X_{t}=o_{p}(Y_{t})
if
X_{t}/Y_{t}\to_{p}0,
where
\to_{p}
denotes convergence in probability. The reader can refer to Definition 1.9 of
Shao 2003
for a detailed introduction to these notations. For a random variable
X\in\mathbf{R}
and a real number
m\geq 1,
define its
m
-norm
\|X\|_{m}=\mathbf{E}\left[|X|^{m}\right]^{1/m}.
We use the notations
\vee,\wedge
to represent the “minimum” and “maximum” of two numbers, i.e.,
a\wedge b=\min(a,b),

a\vee b=\max(a,b).
We use
C,C_{0},C_{1},C_{2},\cdots
to represent general constants, whose values are independent of the sample size
T.

In the manuscript, the bold (Greek) lower-case letters, such as
\mathbf{a}
or
\boldsymbol{\omega},
refer to (random) vectors, while the bold (Greek) upper-case letters, such as
\mathbf{A}
or
\boldsymbol{\Omega},
refer to (random) matrices. Superscripts in brackets represent the specific elements of the vector. For example, a vector
\mathbf{a}=\left(\mathbf{a}^{(1)},\cdots,\mathbf{a}^{(d)}\right)^{\top}\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
and a matrix
\mathbf{A}=\left\{\mathbf{A}^{(i,j)}\right\}_{i=1,\cdots,d_{1},j=1,\cdots,d_{2}}\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}.
For a vector
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
and a number
m\geq 1,
we denote its
m
-norm
|\mathbf{a}|_{m}=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}|\mathbf{a}^{(j)}|^{m}\right)^{1/m}
and
|\mathbf{a}|_{\infty}=\max_{j=1,\cdots,d}\left|\mathbf{a}^{(j)}\right|.
For a
d\times d
matrix, we denote its spectral norm by
|\mathbf{A}|_{2}
as its largest singular value, and its Frobenius norm
|\mathbf{A}|_{F}=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{A}^{(i,j)2}}.
For a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbf{R}^{d\times d},
define the trace of
\mathbf{A}
by
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathbf{A}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{A}^{(j,j)}.

2
Setting and methodology

This section introduces the bimodal weighted-average test statistic, along with a corresponding dependent wild bootstrap algorithm for hypothesis testing.

2.1
Constructing the test statistic

Suppose the observed time series
\mathbf{z}_{t}=\left(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}\right)^{\top}\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}+d_{2}}
satisfies
\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t}\right]=0,
and the objective is to test the independence between the sequences
\left\{\mathbf{x}_{t}\right\}_{t\in\mathbf{Z}}
and
\left\{\mathbf{y}_{t}\right\}_{t\in\mathbf{Z}}.
Unlike in the independent-data setting
Gretton et al. 2007
,
Albert et al. 2022
, the presence of nonzero autocovariances in time series induces bias in the test statistic, inflating its magnitude even under
H_{0}.
Existing literature, such as
Robbins & Fisher 2015
,
Wang et al. 2021
removed the autocovariances by
whitening the data via fitting a vector autoregressive model. However, such methods are generally unsuitable for nonstationary time series.

This work attenuates autocovariances by excluding pairs with small time lags. Specifically, for two given integer bandwidths
1<\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<T
and a given lag
h\geq 0,
we define the test statistic as

\displaystyle\widehat{R}=\sum_{t=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}O_{t},\hskip 10.00002ptO_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{U}}\sum_{t_{1}=(t-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t-\lambda_{1}}\sum_{s=0}^{h\wedge(t_{1}-1)}\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}\mathbf{y}_{t-s}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}-s}

(2.1)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\mathcal{U}}\sum_{t_{1}=(t-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t-\lambda_{1}}\sum_{s=1}^{h\wedge(t_{1}-1)}\mathbf{x}_{t-s}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}-s}\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}},

where
\mathcal{U}=2\sqrt{d_{1}d_{2}T(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})h}
normalizes
\widehat{R}
to remain order
O_{p}(1)
under
H_{0}
. As in
Robbins & Fisher 2015
,
Wang et al. 2021
, we sum over lags
s
up to
h
to detect serial dependence up to lag
h.
Allowing
h
to diverge with
T
enables the test statistic to capture cross-series dependence over increasingly long lags. We further demonstrate in Remark
1
that a sufficiently large
\lambda_{1}
reduces the bias induced by temporal dependence. The idea of excluding pairs to mitigate bias has also been explored in the literature, for example in
Chen & Qin 2010
,
Chen et al. 2019
in the context of independent data.

We refer to
\widehat{R}
as “bimodal weighted-average” because
\mathcal{U}\widehat{R}
can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=1}^{T}f(t_{1}-t_{2})\sum_{s=0}^{h\wedge(t_{1}\wedge t_{2}-1)}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{2}}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}-s}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{2}-s}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=1}^{T}f(t_{1}-t_{2})\sum_{s=1}^{h\wedge(t_{1}\wedge t_{2}-1)}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}-s}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{2}-s}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{2}},

where
f(x)=1
if
\lambda_{1}\leq|x|\leq\lambda_{2},
and
f(x)=0
otherwise. Viewing
f(\cdot)
as a kernel function, it is even and exhibits a bimodal shape: it vanishes at the origin and for large
|x|
, while attaining its maximum at intermediate values in
(0,\infty).
This contrasts with standard kernel functions, which typically peak at zero and decay as
|x|
increases. In this sense, the proposed test statistic is constructed using a piecewise constant kernel
f.

Remarks
1
and
2
derive the expectations of the test statistic under both
H_{0}
and
H_{1},
thereby explaining why
\widehat{R}
distinguishes between temporal dependence and dependence across two time series.

Remark 1

(Under
H_{0}
)
.

Under independence between the sequences
\{\mathbf{x}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbf{Z}}
and
\{\mathbf{y}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbf{Z}},

temporal dependence dominates the bias of
\widehat{R}.
We demonstrate in Remark
6
of the online supplement that the expectation of
\widehat{R}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\mathbf{E}\left[\widehat{R}\right]\right|\leq\frac{CTd_{1}d_{2}h}{\mathcal{U}\lambda_{1}^{2\alpha-1}}

(2.2)

under
H_{0}.
Therefore, for sufficiently large
\lambda_{1},
the bias arising from temporal dependence becomes asymptotically negligible compared to its stochastic error.

Remark 2

(Under
H_{1}
)
.

Under the alternative hypothesis, due to dependence between the two time series,
\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}\mathbf{y}_{t-s}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}-s}\right]\neq\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}\right]\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{y}_{t-s}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}-s}\right]
in general. For any
t\in\mathbf{Z}
and
s\geq 0,
we define the cross-covariance matrices
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,1}=\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{t}\mathbf{y}_{t-s}^{\top}\right]\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
and
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,2}=\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{y}_{t}\mathbf{x}_{t-s}^{\top}\right]\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{2}\times d_{1}}.
We demonstrate in Remark
7
of the online supplement that the expectation
\mathbf{E}\left[O_{t}\right]
contains contributions involving the products
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t_{1},s,1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,1}^{\top}\right)
and
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t_{1},s,2}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,2}^{\top}\right),
where
\mathrm{Tr}(\cdot)
denotes the trace of a matrix. Consequently, the proposed statistic accumulates these nonzero contributions, leading to a non-negligible bias under
H_{1}.
This mechanism allows the test to distinguish cross-series dependence from temporal dependence within each series.

Remark
2
also highlights the effect of nonstationarity. If the time series
\mathbf{x}_{t}
and
\mathbf{y}_{t}
have stationary autocovariances, then
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,1}=\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{1,s,1}
for all
t,
and

\mathrm{Tr}\left(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t_{1},s,1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,1}^{\top}\right)=\mathrm{Tr}\left(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{1,s,1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{1,s,1}^{\top}\right)\geq 0,

which makes
\mathbf{E}\left[O_{t}\right]
positive. In contrast, without autocovariance stationarity, this bias can be negative, and a two-sided (rather than one-sided) alternative is required, which is different from the tests for stationary time series considered in
Robbins & Fisher 2015
.

Figure
1
plots the dynamics of the bias of the test statistic under both
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
, in agreement with Remarks
1
and
2
. Under the null, the bias is driven by the autocovariances, and increasing
\lambda_{1}
renders this bias negligible. In contrast, under
H_{1}
, the presence of nonzero cross-covariance matrices induces extra bias in the test statistic, thereby inflating its absolute value.

(a)

\log(\mathrm{Bias})
under
H_{0}
.

(b)

\log(\mathrm{Bias})
under
H_{1}
.

Figure 1
:
\log(\mathrm{Bias})
of the test statistic under
H_{0}
and
H_{1}.
Data are generated from the “nAR(1)” process in Section
4.2
. Each value is calculated from 5000 independent replications. Blue dots denote the estimated bias, and red lines indicate the fitted regression line.

2.2
Bootstrap-assisted hypothesis testing

The complex autocovariance structure of nonstationary time series induces a nontrivial variance for the test statistic. To circumvent explicit variance estimation, we develop a bootstrap procedure that approximates the sampling distribution of the test statistic through simulation.

The literature offers a wide range of bootstrap algorithms for statistical inference with time series data. Examples include
Politis & Romano 1994
,
Paparoditis & Politis 2001
,
Paparoditis & Politis 2002
,
Tewes et al. 2019
for general stationary time series;
Bühlmann 1997
,
Meyer & Kreiss 2015
,
Jentsch & Politis 2015
for linear processes; and
Zhou 2015
,
Wu & Zhou 2018
for nonstationary time series.
Shao 2010
introduced the dependent wild bootstrap for stationary time series, and subsequent works, such as
Zhang & Politis 2023
,
Zhang et al. 2024
, have extended it to accommodate nonstationary settings.

The classical dependent wild bootstrap was primarily designed for linear combinations of observations (such as sample mean) and therefore could not fully capture higher-order cumulant information. More recent studies, including
Zhang et al. 2024
,
Zhang 2025
, have extended the algorithm to account for fourth-order cumulants.
Building on these advances, we employ a second-order dependent wild bootstrap for hypothesis testing.

The implementation of the bootstrap algorithm needs a kernel function that satisfies the following conditions.

Definition 1

(Kernel function)
.

Suppose the function
K(\cdot):\mathbf{R}\to[0,\infty)
satisfies
K(0)=1,

K(\cdot)
is decreasing on
[0,\infty)
and is symmetric around
0,
and
\sup_{x\in\mathbf{R}}|K^{\prime}(x)|<\infty.
Assume that
\int_{\mathbf{R}}K(x)\mathrm{d}x<\infty.
For any
t\in\mathbf{R},
define the Fourier transform of
K(\cdot)
as
\mathcal{F}K(t)=\int_{\mathbf{R}}K(x)\exp(-2\pi\mathrm{i}tx)\mathrm{d}x.
We assume that
\mathcal{F}K(t)\geq 0
for all
t\in\mathbf{R}
and
\int_{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{F}K(t)\mathrm{d}t<\infty.

A canonical example is the Gaussian kernel
K(x)=\exp\left(-\frac{x^{2}}{2}\right),
which satisfies the conditions of Definition
1
.

Remark 3
.

The conditions on the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}K
ensure that the covariance matrix induced by the kernel is positive semi-definite. Specifically, for any real numbers
c_{t},t=1,\cdots,T,
from Theorem 8.26 of
Folland 1999
, for any variance bandwidth
\mathcal{K}_{T}>0,

\displaystyle\sum_{t_{1}=1}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=1}^{T}c_{t_{1}}c_{t_{2}}K\left(\frac{t_{1}-t_{2}}{\mathcal{K}_{T}}\right)=\int_{\mathbf{R}}\mathcal{F}K(y)\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}c_{t}\exp\left(\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}yt_{1}}{\mathcal{K}_{T}}\right)\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}y\geq 0,

so the matrix
\left\{K\left(\frac{t_{1}-t_{2}}{\mathcal{K}_{T}}\right)\right\}_{t_{1},t_{2}=1,\cdots,T}
is positive semi-definite.

Input:

Observations
\mathbf{z}_{t}=\left(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}\right)^{\top}\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}+d_{2}}
for
t=1,\cdots,T,
integer bandwidths

1\leq\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<T
and
h\geq 0,
a kernel function
K(\cdot)
satisfying Definition
1
, a variance bandwidth

\mathcal{K}_{T}>0,
number of bootstrap replicates
B,
and the nominal size (Type-I error)
0<\mathcal{A}<1.

Derive the test statistic
\widehat{R}
as well as
O_{t}
for
t=1+\lambda_{1},\cdots,T
as in equation (
2.1
).

for

b=1

to

B

do

Generate joint normal random variables
\left(e^{*}_{1},\cdots,e^{*}_{T}\right)
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[e^{*}_{t}\right]=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathrm{Cov}\left(e^{*}_{t_{1}},e^{*}_{t_{2}}\right)=K\left(\frac{t_{1}-t_{2}}{\mathcal{K}_{T}}\right).

Calculate the linear combination
\widehat{R}^{*}_{b}=\sum_{t=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}O_{t}e^{*}_{t}.

Calculate the
1-\alpha
sample quantile
q^{*}_{1-\alpha}
of
\left|\widehat{R}^{*}_{b}\right|:
Sort
\left|\widehat{R}^{*}_{b}\right|
into
\left|\widehat{R}^{*}_{(1)}\right|\leq\left|\widehat{R}^{*}_{(2)}\right|\leq\cdots\leq\left|\widehat{R}^{*}_{(B)}\right|,
and the sample quantile is given by
\left|\widehat{R}^{*}_{(q^{*})}\right|,
where
q^{*}
is the smallest number such that
q^{*}/B\geq 1-\mathcal{A}.

Output:

Reject
H_{0}
if
\left|\widehat{R}\right|>q^{*}_{1-\mathcal{A}}
.

Algorithm 1

Second-order dependent wild bootstrap for hypothesis testing.

The use of a two-sided rejection region is necessary due to the effect of autocovariance nonstationarity discussed in Remark
2
, under which the bias need not be positive.

To elucidate the mechanism of Algorithm
1
, note that
e^{*}_{1},\cdots,e^{*}_{T}
have joint normal distribution, so
\widehat{R}^{*}_{b},
which is a linear combination of
e^{*}_{t},
also has normal distribution in the bootstrap world (conditional on the observed data). Furthermore, the mean and variance of
\widehat{R}^{*}_{b}
in the bootstrap world are given by 0 and

\sum_{t_{1}=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}O_{t_{1}}O_{t_{2}}K\left(\frac{t_{1}-t_{2}}{\mathcal{K}_{T}}\right)

respectively.
Therefore, Algorithm
1
actually generates mean-zero normal random variables with a specific variance. Furthermore, as demonstrated in Theorem
3.3
, the test statistic has an asymptotic mean-zero normal distribution under
H_{0}.
Hence, the distribution of
\widehat{R}^{*}_{b}
in the bootstrap world approximates that of
\widehat{R},
provided that its variance is close to the asymptotic variance of
\widehat{R}.
This variance consistency is proved in (
\thechapter.D.49
) of the online supplement.

Remark 4
.

The variance of
\widehat{R}^{*}_{b}
in the bootstrap world serves as a heteroskedasticity and autocorrelation consistent (HAC) estimator of the asymptotic variance of
\widehat{R}
. HAC estimators, originating in
Newey & West 1987
,
Andrews 1991
, have proven useful for heterogeneous data and nonstationary time series; see, for example,
Shao 2010
,
Zhou 2015
,
Ding & Zhou 2020
,
Wang & Sun 2022
,
Sun & Wang 2022
,
Zhang & Politis 2023
.
In our setting, where no parametric structure is imposed on fourth-order cumulants, HAC estimation provides a natural and flexible approach for estimating variance.

3
Asymptotic results

This section establishes the asymptotic distribution of the test statistic under
H_{0},
as well as the consistency of Algorithm
1
. To this end, we introduce a class of short-range dependence conditions, named
(M,\alpha)
-short-range dependence, which accommodates nonlinear and nonstationary data-generating processes. Under these conditions, we derive concentration inequalities for quadratic forms of high-dimensional time series. These results are of independent interest, as many statistics in the time series literature—including sample covariance and precision matrices, spectral density
Zhang & Wu 2021
, and sample autoregressive coefficient matrices
Guo et al. 2016
,
Zhang 2023
—can be reduced to the analysis of quadratic forms. Consequently, the developments in Section
3.1
may be of broader interest beyond the scope of this paper.

3.1
A short-range dependence condition

Suppose
\left\{e_{t}\in\mathbf{H},t\in\mathbf{Z}\right\}
are independent (but not necessarily identically distributed) random variables taking values in
\mathbf{H}
, where
\mathbf{H}
is a measurable space. We assume that

\mathbf{z}_{t}=g_{t,T}\left(\cdots,e_{t-2},e_{t-1}\right),

(3.1)

where
g_{t,T}(\cdot):\mathbf{H}^{\mathbf{Z}}\to\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}+d_{2}}
is a measurable function. The subscript
t,T
indicates that the function
g_{t,T}(\cdot)
may vary with respect to the time index
t
and the sample size
T.
We define
\left\{e_{t}^{\dagger}\in\mathbf{H},t\in\mathbf{Z}\right\}
as an independent copy of
\left\{e_{t}\in\mathbf{H},t\in\mathbf{Z}\right\}:
the random variable sequence
\left\{e_{t}^{\dagger}\in\mathbf{H},t\in\mathbf{Z}\right\}
is independent of
\left\{e_{t}\in\mathbf{H},t\in\mathbf{Z}\right\},
and
e^{\dagger}_{t}
has the same distribution as
e_{t}
for any
t.
For any integer
s\in\mathbf{Z},
define

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{t,s}=\begin{cases}g_{t,T}\left(\cdots,e_{t-s-2},e_{t-s-1},e_{t-s}^{\dagger},e_{t-s+1},\cdots,e_{t}\right)&\text{if}\quad s\geq 0,\\
\mathbf{z}_{t}&\text{if}\quad s<0.\end{cases}

We define
\delta_{t,s}
and
\delta_{s}
as follows:

\displaystyle\delta_{t,s}=\sup_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}+d_{2}},\ |\mathbf{a}|_{2}=1}\left\|\mathbf{a}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{z}_{t}-\mathbf{z}_{t,s}\right)\right\|_{M},\hskip 10.00002pt\delta_{s}=\sup_{t\in\mathbf{Z}}\delta_{t,s}.

With a slight abuse of notation, we do not explicitly include
M
in
\delta_{t,s}
and
\delta_{s}.
Since
M
is considered as fixed throughout the manuscript, this abuse of notation should not cause confusion. With these notations, we are able to introduce the definition of
(M,\alpha)
-short-range dependence conditions, presented as follows:

Definition 2

(
(M,\alpha)
-short-range dependence)
.

For two given real numbers
M,\alpha>1,
suppose that the random variables
\mathbf{z}_{t},t\in\mathbf{Z}
satisfy equation (
3.1
). Furthermore, we assume that
\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t}\right]=0,

\sup_{t\in\mathbf{Z};\ \mathbf{a}\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}+d_{2}},\ |\mathbf{a}|_{2}=1}\left\|\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}\right\|_{M}=O(1),
and
\sup_{s\geq 0}(1+s)^{\alpha}\sum_{q=s}^{\infty}\delta_{q}=O(1).
Then we say that
\mathbf{z}_{t},t\in\mathbf{Z}
satisfies the
(M,\alpha)
-short-range dependence.

Remark 5
.

Discussions of processes of the form (
3.1
) trace back to a conjecture of N. Wiener, later disproved by
Rosenblatt 2009
.
Wu 2005
introduced a short-range dependence condition for processes of the form (
3.1
). Subsequent studies—such as
Shao & Wu 2007
,
Zhou & Shao 2013
,
Wu & Wu 2016
—have applied this condition to derive distributional results for various statistics. However,
Wu 2005
assumed that the time series
\mathbf{z}_{t}
is strictly stationary, i.e., the joint distribution of
\mathbf{z}_{t+1},\cdots,\mathbf{z}_{t+k}
is identical to the joint distribution of
\mathbf{z}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{z}_{k}
for any
k\geq 1
and any
t\in\mathbf{Z},
as in Definition 1.3.3 of
Brockwell & Davis 1991
.

More recent studies—including
Zhou 2013
,
Wu et al. 2026
,
Zhang et al. 2024
,
Zhang & Wu 2017
,
Zhang & Wu 2021
,
Zhang & Cheng 2018
—have extended the dependence conditions of
Wu 2005
to accommodate nonstationary and high-dimensional time series. Definition
2
also generalizes the conditions of
Wu 2005
to the setup of high-dimensional nonstationary time series, but it
imposes moment bounds on linear combinations
\boldsymbol{a}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t},
rather than on individual entries. This formulation is motivated by the test statistic involving inner products of observations. Related conditions appeared in
Chen & Qin 2010
,
Zhang 2025
, where quadratic-type test statistics were considered.

The literature has offered alternative short-range dependence conditions, including various mixing conditions
Doukhan 1994
and near-epoch dependence (NED)
Adamek et al. 2023
. The process defined in (
3.1
) can be viewed as a special case of an NED process, where the innovations
e_{t}
are independent rather than mixing. As noted in
Wu 2005
, verifying Definition
2
is comparatively simple relative to mixing conditions, as it only requires bounding moments of random variables, whereas mixing conditions typically involve controlling joint probabilities. On the other hand, Definition
2
relies on a nonlinear moving-average representation of the observations, while mixing conditions generally do not impose a specific functional form on the data-generating process.

Example 1
.

Suppose a nonstationary linear process

\mathbf{z}_{t}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{A}_{t,j}\boldsymbol{e}_{t-j},

where
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}+d_{2}}
are independent random vectors (that is, we choose
\mathbf{H}
as
\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}+d_{2}}
with Borel
\sigma
-field) with
\mathbf{E}\left[\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\right]=0
and
\sup_{t\in\mathbf{Z};\ \mathbf{a}\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}+d_{2}},\ |\mathbf{a}|_{2}=1}\left\|\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{e}_{t}\right\|_{M}=O(1)
(like those in
Chen & Qin 2010
). We further assume that
\sup_{t\in\mathbf{Z}}\left|\mathbf{A}_{t,j}\right|_{2}\leq\frac{C}{(1+j)^{\alpha+1}}
for any
j\geq 0.
With these assumptions, for any vector
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}+d_{2}}
with
|\mathbf{a}|_{2}=1,
we have
\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t}\right]=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{A}_{t,j}\mathbf{E}\left[\boldsymbol{e}_{t-j}\right]=0,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}\right\|_{M}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left\|\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t,j}\boldsymbol{e}_{t-j}\right\|_{M}\leq C\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left|\mathbf{A}_{t,j}^{\top}\mathbf{a}\right|_{2}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{C_{1}}{(1+j)^{1+\alpha}}\leq C_{2},

and for any
j\geq 0,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{a}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{z}_{t}-\mathbf{z}_{t,j}\right)\right\|_{M}=\left\|\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t,j}\left(\mathbf{e}_{t-j}-\mathbf{e}_{t-j}^{\dagger}\right)\right\|_{M}\leq 2\left\|\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t,j}\mathbf{e}_{t-j}\right\|_{M}\leq\frac{C}{(1+j)^{1+\alpha}},

which implies

\sum_{j=s}^{\infty}\delta_{q}\leq\sum_{j=s}^{\infty}\frac{C}{(1+j)^{1+\alpha}}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{(1+s)^{\alpha}},

which verifies the
(M,\alpha)
-short-range dependence of
\mathbf{z}_{t}.

Theorem
3.1
presents concentration inequalities in moments of linear combinations and quadratic forms of
\mathbf{z}_{t},
which will serve as the theoretical foundation of the distributional results for the test statistics in later sections. In the following parts of this section, for any
t\in\mathbf{Z}
and
s\geq 0,
we define
\mathcal{F}_{t,s}
as the
\sigma
-field generated by
e_{t},e_{t-1},\cdots,e_{t-s}.

Theorem 3.1
.

Suppose
\mathbf{z}_{t},t\in\mathbf{Z}
satisfy Definition
2
with
M>8
and
\alpha>8,
and the dimension
d=d_{1}+d_{2}=O(T),
where
T
denotes the sample size. Then there exists a constant
C
independent of the sample size
T
and the dimension
d
such that for any real-value vectors
\mathbf{a}_{t}\in\mathbf{R}^{d},t=1,\cdots,T
and any
s\geq 0,

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}\right\|_{M}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{\sum_{t=1}^{T}|\mathbf{a}_{t}|^{2}_{2}},

(3.2)

\displaystyle\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\left\|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{z}_{t}-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,s}\right]\right)\right\|_{M}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(1+s)^{\alpha}}\sqrt{\sum_{t=1}^{T}|\mathbf{a}_{t}|^{2}_{2}}.

Furthermore, assume that two integer bandwidths
1<\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<T
satisfy
\lambda_{1}\asymp T^{\kappa_{1}},

\lambda_{2}\asymp T^{\kappa_{2}},
where
1>\kappa_{2}>\kappa_{1}>\frac{5}{2(\alpha-2)},
then for any real symmetric matrices
\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}},
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{t_{1}=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=(t_{1}-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t_{1}-\lambda_{1}}\left(\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\right]\right)\right\|_{M/2}

(3.3)

\displaystyle=O\left(\sqrt{d\sum_{t_{1}=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=(t_{1}-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t_{1}-\lambda_{1}}\left|\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\right|^{2}_{2}}\right).

Furthermore, there exists a constant
C>0
such that for any integer
S>2\lambda_{2},

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{t_{1}=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=(t_{1}-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t_{1}-\lambda_{1}}\left(\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{1},S}\right]\right)\right\|_{M/2}\leq\frac{CT\sqrt{T}a^{\dagger}}{(S-\lambda_{2})^{\alpha-2}},

(3.4)

where
a^{\dagger}=\max_{t_{1}=1+\lambda_{1},\cdots,T;\ t_{2}=(t_{1}-\lambda_{2})\vee 1,\cdots,t_{1}-\lambda_{1}}|\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}|_{2}.

We note that the moment bound in (
3.3
) remains valid for mutually independent
\mathbf{z}_{t}
with independent entries, as established in
Whittle 1960
; see also Remark
8
in Appendix
\thechapter.D
. Therefore, Theorem
3.1
extends the results of
Whittle 1960
to settings involving dependent random variables.

The differing bounds in (
3.3
) and (
3.4
) reflect the cost of approximating the original quadratic forms by sums of
m
-dependent random variables (see Definition 6.4.3 of
Brockwell & Davis 2006
); the technical details are provided in (
\thechapter.D.10
) in the online supplement. To provide some intuition, suppose that
\mathbf{z}_{t}
is
m
-dependent with
m<S.
In this case,
\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t_{1},S}
-measurable, and

\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{1},S}\right]=\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{1},S}\right]=\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\right]=0

if
S<t_{1}-t_{2}.
This makes
\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{1},S}\right]
a poor approximation when the time lag
t_{1}-t_{2}
is significantly larger than
S.
This crude approximation increases the approximation error of the summation
\sum_{t_{1}=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=(t_{1}-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t_{1}-\lambda_{1}}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{1},S}\right]
in (
3.4
).

Corollary 3.2
.

From equation (
3.2
), suppose
t_{1}>t_{2},

\displaystyle\left|\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{2}}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d_{1}}\left|\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}\right]\right)\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}^{(i)}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d_{1}}\left\|\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}^{(i)}\right\|_{M}\left\|\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}\right]\right\|_{M}\leq\frac{Cd_{1}}{(t_{1}-t_{2})^{\alpha}},

and

\displaystyle\left|\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{2}}\right]\right|\leq\sum_{i=d_{1}+1}^{d}\left|\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{(i)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}\right]\right)\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}^{(i)}\right]\right|\leq\frac{Cd_{2}}{(t_{1}-t_{2})^{\alpha}}.

Therefore, in a heuristic sense, Definition
2
ensures that the temporal correlations of
\mathbf{x}_{t}
and
\mathbf{y}_{t}
decrease at a polynomial rate. The parameter
\alpha
quantifies the strength of dependence: a smaller
\alpha
corresponds to a slower decay of covariances, indicating potentially strong dependence.

3.2
Asymptotic distribution under
H_{0}

By leveraging concentration inequalities developed in Section
3.1
, this section establishes the asymptotic distribution of the test statistic and the validity of the bootstrap algorithm under the null hypothesis, which controls the Type-I error of the test.

Theorem
3.3
builds on ideas from
Chernozhukov et al. 2013
,
Zhang & Cheng 2018
,
Xu et al. 2019
in controlling approximation errors, but differs from these works in that it derives asymptotic distributional approximations for quadratic forms of dependent data.

Theorem 3.3
.

Suppose Assumptions
\thechapter.D.1
,
\thechapter.D.2
,
\thechapter.D.3
of Appendix
\thechapter.D.1
hold true. Then we have

\sup_{x\in\mathbf{R}}\left|\mathbf{pr}\left(\widehat{R}\leq x\right)-\mathbf{pr}\left(\epsilon\leq x\right)\right|=o(1),

(3.5)

where the random variable
\epsilon
has normal distribution with
\mathbf{E}\left[\epsilon\right]=0
and
\mathrm{Var}\left(\epsilon\right)=\mathrm{Var}\left(\widehat{R}^{\dagger}\right),
and
\widehat{R}^{\dagger}
is the canonical statistic defined in Assumption
\thechapter.D.3
of Appendix
\thechapter.D.1
.

Theorem
3.3
further justifies the claim in Remark
1
: For appropriate choices of
\lambda_{1},
the test statistic has an asymptotic normal distribution with mean zero. The asymptotic normality also makes it reasonable to sample jointly normal random variables in Algorithm
1
.

The validity of Algorithm
1
hinges on the fact that the distribution of the bootstrapped statistics
\widehat{R}^{*}_{b}
in the bootstrap world accurately approximates the distribution of
\widehat{R}.
Specifically, define the “probability in the bootstrap world”—that is, the conditional probability conditional on all observations—as
\mathbf{pr}^{*}\left(\cdot\right)=\mathbf{pr}\left(\cdot\mid\mathbf{z}_{t},t=1,\cdots,T\right).
According to Theorem 1.2.1 of
Politis et al. 1999
, it suffices to establish that

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbf{R}}\left|\mathbf{pr}^{*}\left(\widehat{R}^{*}_{b}\leq x\right)-\mathbf{pr}\left(\epsilon\leq x\right)\right|=o_{p}(1),

(3.6)

where
\epsilon
is defined in Theorem
3.3
. We demonstrate this result in Theorem
3.4
.

Theorem 3.4
.

Suppose Assumptions
\thechapter.D.1
,
\thechapter.D.2
,
\thechapter.D.3
, and
\thechapter.D.4
of Appendix
\thechapter.D.1
hold true. Then (
3.6
) holds true.

4
Numerical simulations

4.1
Implementation details

The proposed testing procedure involves four bandwidth parameters:
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
,
h
, and
\mathcal{K}_{T}
. Among these bandwidths,
h
determines the maximal lag up to which mutual dependence is captured; as discussed in
Hong 1996
,
Duchesne & Roy 2003
,
Shao 2009
,
Robbins & Fisher 2015
. The parameter
\mathcal{K}_{T}

corresponds to the bandwidth used in HAC estimation, which is common in the literature; see, for example,
Shao 2010
,
Ding & Zhou 2020
,
Zhang & Politis 2023
. The literature has proposed various methods for selecting
\mathcal{K}_{T}
in different kinds of bootstrap settings
Hall et al. 1995
,
Lahiri 2005
,
Lahiri et al. 2007
. In particular, the original dependent wild bootstrap of
Shao 2010
leveraged
Politis & White 2004
for bandwidth selection.

In contrast,
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
are associated with the proposed test statistic
\widehat{R}.
The parameter
\lambda_{1}
serves to mitigate the bias induced by temporal dependence, while
\lambda_{2}
controls the dependence strength in the products of time series data. Algorithm
2
provides a data-driven method for selecting
\lambda_{1}.
We then set
\lambda_{2}=\iota\lambda_{1},
with different scale parameters
\iota.
Numerical experiments indicate that the size of the proposed test remains stable across a wide range of
\lambda_{2}.
However, the power can be affected if
\lambda_{2}
is chosen to be excessively small or excessively large.

The design of Algorithm
2
is motivated by the need to control the temporal bias of the test statistic.
To illustrate this, suppose the null hypothesis holds true and assume that
\mathbf{z}_{t},t\in\mathbf{Z}
is weak stationary. From equation (
\thechapter.B.1
) in the online supplement, if we set
h=0,
then

\mathcal{U}\left|\mathbf{E}\left[O_{t}\right]\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t_{1}=(t-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t-\lambda_{1}}\left(\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}\right]\right)^{2}+\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}}\right]\right)^{2}\right).

Therefore, Algorithm
2
chooses the smallest
\lambda_{1}
such that
\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}\right]\right)^{2}+\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}}\right]\right)^{2}
is small compared to the total sum of squared sample autocovariances. This choice ensures a small temporal bias. Although
\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}\right]
and
\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}}\right]
are not observable, under stationarity they can be estimated by the sample autocovariances
\widehat{\sigma}_{t-t_{1}}^{(x)}
and
\widehat{\sigma}_{t-t_{1}}^{(y)}
in Algorithm
2
, leading to the design of Algorithm
2
.

Input:

Observations
\mathbf{z}_{t}=\left(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}\right)^{\top}\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}+d_{2}}
for
t=1,\cdots,T,
ratio threshold
\mathcal{F}.

Derive the estimators
\widehat{\sigma}_{a}^{(x)}=\left(\sum_{t=a+1}^{T}\mathbf{x}^{\top}_{t}\mathbf{x}_{t-a}\right)/T
and
\widehat{\sigma}_{a}^{(y)}=\left(\sum_{t=a+1}^{T}\mathbf{y}^{\top}_{t}\mathbf{y}_{t-a}\right)/T

for
a=0,1,\cdots,T-1.

Choose
\lambda_{1}
as the smallest integer such that

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{a=\lambda_{1}}^{T-1}\left(\widehat{\sigma}_{a}^{(x)2}+\widehat{\sigma}_{a}^{(y)2}\right)}{\sum_{a=0}^{T-1}\left(\widehat{\sigma}_{a}^{(x)2}+\widehat{\sigma}_{a}^{(y)2}\right)}\leq\mathcal{F}

Output:

The bandwidth
\lambda_{1}
.

Remark:
We set the ratio threshold to
\mathcal{F}=0.5\%
in our experiments.

Algorithm 2

Selection of
\lambda_{1}.

This experiment sets the scale parameter
\iota
to
\lfloor 2T^{0.05}\rfloor,\lfloor 2T^{0.1}\rfloor,\lfloor 2T^{0.2}\rfloor,
with
\lfloor x\rfloor
denotes the largest integer that is smaller than or equal to
x.
Notably, the choice
\lfloor 2T^{0.2}\rfloor
violates Assumption
\thechapter.D.4
in the online supplement. We intentionally include this ratio to assess the robustness of the proposed test to violations of the underlying assumptions. Following
Robbins & Fisher 2015
, we consider three choices of
h=\lfloor\log(T)\rfloor,\lfloor 6T^{0.1}\rfloor,\lfloor 6T^{0.3}\rfloor.

This experiment adopts the method proposed in
Politis & White 2004
, implemented in the R package
blocklength
, to select
\mathcal{K}_{T}
. Since the procedure in
Politis & White 2004
is designed for univariate time series, we apply it to the scalar series
\mathbf{z}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}
for
t=1,\ldots,T
.

4.2
Simulation data

The numerical experiment considers three data-generating processes of
\mathbf{z}_{t}=(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top})^{\top}\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}+d_{2}},
namely

•

nonstationary autoregressive (nAR(1)) process:

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{t}=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}+\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}_{t-1}&\text{if}\quad t\mod 2=1,\\
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{z}_{t-1}&\text{if}\quad t\mod 2=0.\end{cases}

•

nonstationary autoregressive moving-average (nARMA(1,1)) process:

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{t}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}+\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t-1}&\text{if}\quad t\mod 2=1,\\
\mathbf{B}\mathbf{z}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}+\mathbf{B}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t-1}&\text{if}\quad t\mod 2=0.\end{cases}

•

nonstationary threshold ARMA (nTARMA(1,1)) process :

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{t}=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}+\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}_{t-1}+\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t-1},&\text{if}\quad t\mod 2=1\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{z}_{t-2}^{(1)}\leq 0,\\
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}_{t-1}-\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t-1},&\text{if}\quad t\mod 2=1\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{z}_{t-2}^{(1)}>0,\\
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}+\mathbf{B}\mathbf{z}_{t-1}+\mathbf{B}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t-1},&\text{if}\quad t\mod 2=0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{z}_{t-2}^{(1)}\leq 0,\\
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}-\mathbf{B}\mathbf{z}_{t-1}-\mathbf{B}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t-1},&\text{if}\quad t\mod 2=0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{z}_{t-2}^{(1)}>0.\\
\end{cases}

The innovations satisfy
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}=\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t},
where
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t}=(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t}^{(1)},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t}^{(d_{1}+d_{2})})^{\top},
and each
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t}^{(j)},t\in\mathbf{Z},j=1,\cdots,d_{1}+d_{2}
satisfy independent
t
-distribution with 20 degrees of freedom. We consider
t
-distribution because it has finite polynomial moments but lacks exponential moments, which satisfies the setup of Section
3
. The values of the matrices
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\Pi}
are postponed to Section
\thechapter.C.1
of the online supplement.

4.3
Simulation results

We choose the sample size
T=300,500,700
and the dimensions
d_{1}=1.2T,d_{2}=1.5T.
The numerical results for
T=500
are reported in Table
1
, and other results are postponed to Table
5
of the online supplement. We compare our proposed method with the HSIC-based testing procedure of
Wang et al. 2021
. As shown in Table
1
, the size of the proposed hypothesis testing procedure is insensitive to different choices of
\lambda_{2}
and
h.
In particular, even when the choices of
\lambda_{2}
and
h
are chosen to be of higher order than required by Assumption
\thechapter.D.3
, the test sizes remain close to the nominal level.

However, appropriate choices of
h
and
\lambda_{2}
can improve the power performance. From Table
1
, when
\lambda_{2}
is set to a moderate level (
\lfloor 2T^{0.1}\rfloor\lambda_{1}
in the experiment), the proposed hypothesis testing procedure achieves the most favorable power performance overall compared to other choices. On the other hand, selecting values of
\lambda_{2}
that are either too small or too large leads to a deterioration in power. Numerical results also indicate that adopting a relatively large bandwidth
h
is beneficial for improving power performance.

Table
1
further highlights the necessity of applying independence tests directly to the time series data
\mathbf{z}_{t},
rather than to fitted residuals, in nonstationary settings. The issue arises from potential model incompatibility and is further illustrated by comparison with Table
2
, where the data-generating process follows a stationary AR(1) model of the form
\mathbf{z}_{t}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{z}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t}.

Specifically, when the time series exhibits a stationary autocovariance structure, practitioners can employ parametric models—such as vector ARMA processes—to remove autocovariances by computing fitted residuals. In contrast, for nonstationary time series with evolving covariance structures, few models are capable of adequately capturing the underlying dependence. As a result, model misspecification induces residual autocorrelation, which in turn deteriorates the size and power performance of the HSIC-based testing procedure proposed in
Wang et al. 2021
.

When the data-generating process exhibits nonlinearity, the size of the test remains stable, whereas its power is affected. Table
6
further illustrates that relatively large sample sizes are required to achieve satisfactory power under nonstationarity. These findings suggest the need for developing independence tests that are suitable for capturing nonlinear dependence structures, for example by exploiting reproducing kernel methods as in
Gretton et al. 2005
, which we leave for future research.

Necessity of selecting a reasonably large
\lambda_{1}.
Equation (
\thechapter.B.3
) suggests that, under
H_{0},
choosing a positive
\lambda_{1}
removes the bias induced by temporal dependence. This theoretical implication is further supported by the numerical results in Table
3
, where we perform hypothesis testing with different values of
\lambda_{1}.

When
\lambda_{1}=0,
the bias arising from temporal dependence dominates the stochastic error, causing the test to reject
H_{0}
regardless of the underlying hypothesis. When
\lambda_{1}
is nonzero but relatively small, the temporal dependence bias still affects the test size—it even partially offsets the bias induced under
H_{1},
resulting in power that can be lower than the size.

5
Applications

This section illustrates the practical use of the proposed test in two applications. The first facilitates causal discovery by refining causal directions after applying causal discovery algorithms (e.g., PC algorithm in Section 5.4.2 of
Spirtes et al. 2012
). The second applies the test to financial data to detect cross-sector dependence in stock returns.

Causal discovery.
After observing a time series
\mathbf{z}_{t}=\left(\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}\right)^{\top}
for
t=1,\cdots,T,

one goal of causal discovery involves determining the direction of causality—whether
\mathbf{x}_{t}
causes
\mathbf{y}_{t}
or
\mathbf{y}_{t}
causes
\mathbf{x}_{t}
—as studied in
Hoyer et al. 2008
,
Huang et al. 2020
.
Zheng et al. 2024
demonstrated that
independence tests could complement causal discovery algorithms, such as the PC algorithm in
Spirtes et al. 2012
, when causal directions were not identifiable.
Hoyer et al. 2008
proposed a three-step procedure for causal discovery: 1. Test if
\mathbf{x}_{t}
and
\mathbf{y}_{t}
are independent; 2. If independence is rejected, fit the model
\mathbf{y}_{t}=\mathbf{f}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t}\right)+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t,1},
estimate
\widehat{\mathbf{f}},
and test independence between
\mathbf{x}_{t}
and the residuals
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{t,1}=\mathbf{y}_{t}-\widehat{\mathbf{f}}(\mathbf{x}_{t}).
Failure to reject the null hypothesis suggests the causal direction
\mathbf{x}_{t}\to\mathbf{y}_{t}.
3. Repeat Step 2 with the reversed model
\mathbf{x}_{t}=\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{y}_{t})+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t,2}
. This section applies our method to infer causal directions in time series data. We generate 4 mutually independent nAR(1) processes
\mathbf{x}_{t,i}
with
i=1,\cdots,4.
After that, define the observations
\mathbf{w}_{t,i},i=1,2,3,4
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{t,1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}_{t,1},\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{w}_{t,2}=\mathbf{A}^{(x)}\mathbf{w}_{t,1}+\mathbf{x}_{t,2},\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{w}_{t,3}=\mathbf{B}^{(x)}\mathbf{w}_{t,1}+\mathbf{x}_{t,3},

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{t,4}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{B}^{(x)}\mathbf{w}_{t,2}+\mathbf{A}^{(x)}\mathbf{w}_{t,3}+\mathbf{x}_{t,4},\hskip 10.00002pt

where
\mathbf{A}^{(x)},\mathbf{B}^{(x)}
are defined in Section
\thechapter.C.1
of the online supplement.
This construction induces the causal graph shown in Figure
2(a)
.

We apply the PC algorithm as implemented in
Zheng et al. 2024
to recover causal relationships among the observations. Since the algorithm is designed for scalar data, we reduce dimensionality at each node via principal component analysis.
The resulting graph is displayed in Figure
2(b)
. Due to temporal dependence and information loss during dimension reduction, the original PC algorithm includes a spurious edge and fails to correctly identify causal directions.

Following
Hoyer et al. 2008
, we use Lasso to estimate
\mathbf{f}
and
\mathbf{g},
and apply Algorithm
1
to calibrate the direction of causality. The results, reported in Table
4
and Figure
2
, show that the calibrated directions align with the true causal graph. However, the spurious edge
W_{3}\to W_{2}
cannot be removed because of the presence of the common confounder
W_{1};
in this case, regression adjustment fails to eliminate the induced dependence between
\mathbf{w}_{t,3}
and
\mathbf{w}_{t,2}.

(a)

Population causal graph

(b)

Causal graph by PC algorithm

(c)

Calibrated causal graph

Figure 2
:
Population and estimated causal graphs for the data-generating process (
5.1
). “
p
” in Figure
2(c)
denotes the empirical P-values (ratio of bootstrapped statistics greater than the test statistic) of Algorithm
1
in testing the corresponding direction.

Testing for independence among stock returns.
This application collects 100 days of daily S&P 500 stock prices obtained from
Kaggle
(see
https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/florentbaptist/sp-500
). After collecting the stock prices, we compute log returns and then group stocks according to their Global Industry Classification Standard (GICS) sector classifications. Our objective is to test for independence between log returns across different sectors.

Figure
3(a)
displays the log returns of two representative stocks, and Figure
3(b)
presents the pairwise hypothesis testing results. The tuning parameters are set to

(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},h,K_{T})=(2,6,9,7.8).

The test results indicate dependence between the communication services and the information technology sectors, which is economically plausible given their business overlap. For instance, companies such as Alphabet Inc. (communication services) are closely related to firms such as Apple Inc. (information technology) through shared platforms and cloud-based services, as discussed in
Cennamo 2021
. We also observe dependence among the health care, information technology, and financial sectors. This pattern may reflect the growing integration of cloud computing and AI-driven technologies in health care and financial services, as demonstrated in
Athey & Stern 2000
,
Gu et al. 2020
. In empirical studies, detecting dependence across time series provides useful empirical evidence and motivates further investigation into related areas.

(a)

Observation examples

(b)

Hypothesis testing results

Figure 3
:
Observation examples and hypothesis testing results on S&P 500 stock prices. The empirical P-values are computed in the same way as in Figure
2
.

Appendix
\thechapter
.A
Additional literature review

High-dimensional vector autoregressive (VAR) processes.
As demonstrated in
Robbins & Fisher 2015
,
Wang et al. 2021
, one possible approach is to whiten the time series by fitting a VAR model and conducting hypothesis testing based on the resulting residuals. However, this approach encounters substantial difficulties in high-dimensional settings. Consistent estimation of VAR coefficient matrices typically relies on structural assumptions, such as sparsity or low-rank conditions; see, for example,
Han et al. 2015
,
Basu & Michailidis 2015
,
Guo et al. 2016
,
Basu et al. 2019
,
Krampe et al. 2023
,
Zhang 2023
,
Enikeeva et al. 2025
. Furthermore, when the time series lacks a stationary autocovariance structure, which is common in modern applications as demonstrated in
Das & Politis 2021
, the fitted residuals may fail to approximate the underlying innovations. Consequently, the whitening step can be unreliable, undermining the validity of subsequent independence tests.

Appendix
\thechapter
.B
Behaviors of the test statistic under
H_{0}
and
H_{1}

Remarks
6
and
7
extend Remarks
1
and
2
in the manuscript by providing detailed calculations of the expectation of the test statistic under both
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
. These calculations formally justify the claims made in those remarks.

Remark 6

(Under
H_{0}
)
.

Under
H_{0},

\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}
is independent of
\mathbf{y}_{t-s}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}-s},
so we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[O_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{U}}\sum_{t_{1}=(t-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t-\lambda_{1}}\sum_{s=0}^{h\wedge(t_{1}-1)}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}\right]\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{y}_{t-s}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}-s}\right]

(
\thechapter
.B.1)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\mathcal{U}}\sum_{t_{1}=(t-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t-\lambda_{1}}\sum_{s=1}^{h\wedge(t_{1}-1)}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{t-s}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}-s}\right]\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}}\right].

From Corollary
3.2
of Section
3
below, under weak dependence (Definition
2
), the covariances
\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{2}}\right]
and
\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{2}}\right]
respectively satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{2}}\right]\right|\leq\frac{Cd_{1}}{|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{\alpha}},\hskip 10.00002pt\left|\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{2}}\right]\right|\leq\frac{Cd_{2}}{|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{\alpha}}.

(
\thechapter
.B.2)

When we take summations, the expectation of
O_{t}
and
\widehat{R}
become

\displaystyle\left|\mathbf{E}\left[O_{t}\right]\right|\leq\frac{Cd_{1}d_{2}}{\mathcal{U}}\sum_{t_{1}=(t-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t-\lambda_{1}}\sum_{s=0}^{h\wedge(t_{1}-1)}\frac{1}{(t-t_{1})^{2\alpha}}\leq\frac{C_{1}d_{1}d_{2}h}{\mathcal{U}\lambda_{1}^{2\alpha-1}},

(
\thechapter
.B.3)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbf{E}\left[\widehat{R}\right]\right|\leq\sum_{t=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}\left|\mathbf{E}\left[O_{t}\right]\right|\leq\frac{CTd_{1}d_{2}h}{\mathcal{U}\lambda_{1}^{2\alpha-1}}.

From (
\thechapter.B.3
), although the test statistic exhibits bias arising from temporal dependence under
H_{0}
, this bias decays at a polynomial rate with respect to the bandwidth
\lambda_{1}.
By selecting a sufficiently large
\lambda_{1},
the bias induced by temporal dependence becomes negligible relative to the stochastic error, and, as demonstrated in Theorem
3.3
, the test statistic remains of constant order.

Remark 7

(Under
H_{1}
)
.

Due to the dependence across two time series,
\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}\mathbf{y}_{t-s}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}-s}\right]\neq\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}\right]\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{y}_{t-s}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}-s}\right]
in general. For any
t\in\mathbf{Z}
and
s\geq 0,
we define the terms
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,1}=\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{t}\mathbf{y}_{t-s}^{\top}\right]\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
and
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,2}=\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{y}_{t}\mathbf{x}_{t-s}^{\top}\right]\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{2}\times d_{1}},
since

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}\mathbf{y}_{t-s}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}-s}\right]=\mathrm{Tr}\left(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t_{1},s,1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,1}^{\top}\right)+\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}-s}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t_{1},s,1}\right)\left(\mathbf{y}_{t-s}\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,1}^{\top}\right)\right],

and we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}\mathbf{E}\left[O_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t_{1}=(t-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t-\lambda_{1}}\sum_{s=0}^{h\wedge(t_{1}-1)}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t_{1},s,1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,1}^{\top}\right)+\sum_{t_{1}=(t-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t-\lambda_{1}}\sum_{s=1}^{h\wedge(t_{1}-1)}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t_{1},s,2}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,2}^{\top}\right)

(
\thechapter
.B.4)

\displaystyle+\sum_{t_{1}=(t-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t-\lambda_{1}}\sum_{s=0}^{h\wedge(t_{1}-1)}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}}\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}-s}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t_{1},s,1}\right)\left(\mathbf{y}_{t-s}\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,1}^{\top}\right)\right]\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{t_{1}=(t-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t-\lambda_{1}}\sum_{s=1}^{h\wedge(t_{1}-1)}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\mathbf{y}_{t_{1}}\mathbf{x}_{t_{1}-s}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t_{1},s,2}\right)\left(\mathbf{x}_{t-s}\mathbf{y}_{t}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,2}^{\top}\right)\right]\right).

Under the alternative where
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t_{1},s,1}\neq 0
or
\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,2}\neq 0
for some
s=0,1,\cdots,h,
the nonzero traces
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t_{1},s,1}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,1}^{\top}\right)
or
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t_{1},s,2}\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{t,s,2}^{\top}\right)
introduce bias to
O_{t}
and consequently into
\widehat{R},
which distinguishes it from
H_{0}.

Appendix
\thechapter
.C
Additional numerical experimental details

\thechapter
.C.1
Values of the parameter matrices in Section
4

We select the matrix

\boldsymbol{\Pi}=\left[\begin{matrix}\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{(x)}&0\\
0&\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{(y)}\end{matrix}\right],

where

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{(x)}=\left[\begin{matrix}1.0&0.5&0&\cdots&0\\
-0.3&1.0&0.5&\cdots&0\\
0&-0.3&1.0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&1.0\end{matrix}\right]\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{1}},\hskip 10.00002pt\boldsymbol{\Pi}^{(y)}=\left[\begin{matrix}1.0&0.3&0&\cdots&0\\
-0.5&1.0&0.3&\cdots&0\\
0&-0.5&1.0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&1.0\end{matrix}\right]\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{2}\times d_{2}}.

Under the null hypothesis, we consider

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{A}^{(x)}&0\\
0&\mathbf{A}^{(y)}\end{matrix}\right],\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{B}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{B}^{(x)}&0\\
0&\mathbf{B}^{(y)}\end{matrix}\right],

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{(x)}=\left[\begin{matrix}0&0.5&-0.2&\cdots&0\\
0&0&0.5&\cdots&0\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\end{matrix}\right]\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{1}},\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{A}^{(y)}=\left[\begin{matrix}0&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0.5&0&0&\cdots&0\\
-0.2&0.5&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\end{matrix}\right]\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{2}\times d_{2}},

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}^{(x)}=\left[\begin{matrix}0&0.2&-0.5&\cdots&0\\
0&0&0.2&\cdots&0\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\end{matrix}\right]\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{1}},\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{B}^{(y)}=\left[\begin{matrix}0&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0.2&0&0&\cdots&0\\
-0.5&0.2&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\end{matrix}\right]\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{2}\times d_{2}}.

Under the alternative hypothesis, we consider

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{A}^{(x)}&\mathbf{A}^{(x,y)}\\
\mathbf{A}^{(x,y)\top}&\mathbf{A}^{(y)}\end{matrix}\right],\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbf{B}=\left[\begin{matrix}\mathbf{B}^{(x)}&\mathbf{B}^{(x,y)}\\
\mathbf{B}^{(x,y)\top}&\mathbf{B}^{(y)}\end{matrix}\right],

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{(x,y)}=\mathbf{B}^{(x,y)}=\left[\begin{matrix}0&\delta&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&\delta&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\end{matrix}\right]\in\mathbf{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}},

where
\delta
reflects the dependence strength.

\thechapter
.C.2
Additional numerical experiments

Table
5
further extends Table
1
in the manuscript by considering different sample sizes. The results show that the empirical size remains stable even when the sample size is relatively small. However, the sample size has a relatively strong effect on the power performance: the power of the test deteriorates for smaller sample sizes.

Influence of high-dimensionality, dimension imbalance, and data nonlinearity.
Table
6
further assesses the effects of high dimensionality, dimensional imbalance, and the nonlinear mutual dependence structure on the performance of the testing procedure.
The results show that both size and power remain stable when the dimension is large relative to the sample size. However, significant dimensional imbalance can lead to a loss of power. In addition, when nonlinear dependence is present, a relatively large sample size is required to maintain satisfactory power.

.

Appendix
\thechapter
.D
Proofs and technical details of Section
3

This section presents the proofs of theoretical results proposed in Section
3
.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Recall that for any
t\in\mathbf{Z}
and
s\geq 0,

\mathcal{F}_{t,s}
is the
\sigma
-field generated by
e_{t},e_{t-1},\cdots,e_{t-s}.

Define

\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,-1}\right]=\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t}\right]=0,

then from Corollary C.9 of
Ø ksendal 2003
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{t}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,j-1}\right]\right),

which implies that

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}\right\|_{M}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left\|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,j-1}\right]\right)\right\|_{M}

and

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{z}_{t}-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,s}\right]\right)\right\|_{M}\leq\sum_{j=s+1}^{\infty}\left\|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,j-1}\right]\right)\right\|_{M}.

For any
j\geq 0
and
z=1,\cdots,T,
define

\displaystyle A_{z,j}=\sum_{t=T-z+1}^{T}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,j-1}\right]\right)

and
\mathcal{A}_{z,j}
the
\sigma
-field generated by
e_{T},e_{T-1},\cdots,e_{T-z+1-j}.
Then
A_{z,j}
is measurable in
\mathcal{A}_{z,j},

\mathcal{A}_{z,j}\subset\mathcal{A}_{z+1,j},
and

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[A_{z+1,j}-A_{z,j}\mid\mathcal{A}_{z,j}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{a}_{T-z}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{T-z}\mid\mathcal{F}_{T-z,j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{a}_{T-z}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{T-z}\mid\mathcal{F}_{T-z,j-1}\right]\right)\mid\mathcal{A}_{z,j}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{a}_{T-z}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{T-z}\mid\mathcal{F}_{T-z,j-1}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{a}_{T-z}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{T-z}\mid\mathcal{F}_{T-z,j-1}\right]=0,

so
A_{z,j}
forms a martingale. According to Theorem 1.1 of
Burkholder et al. 1972
,

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,j-1}\right]\right)\right\|_{M}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\|\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,j-1}\right]\right\|^{2}_{M}}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\|\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}-\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t,j}\right\|^{2}_{M}}\leq C\delta_{j}\sqrt{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left|\mathbf{a}_{t}\right|^{2}_{2}}.

From this inequality,

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t}\right\|_{M}\leq C\sqrt{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left|\mathbf{a}_{t}\right|^{2}_{2}}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\delta_{j}\leq C_{1}\sqrt{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left|\mathbf{a}_{t}\right|^{2}_{2}},

and

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{a}_{t}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{z}_{t}-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,s}\right]\right)\right\|_{M}\leq C\sqrt{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left|\mathbf{a}_{t}\right|^{2}_{2}}\sum_{j=s+1}^{\infty}\delta_{j}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{(1+s)^{\alpha}}\sqrt{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left|\mathbf{a}_{t}\right|^{2}_{2}},

and we prove (
3.2
).

For any
t\in\mathbf{Z}
and any
s\in\mathbf{Z},
define

\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t,s}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,s}\right]&\text{if}\quad s\geq 0,\\
0&\text{if}\quad s<0,\end{cases}\hskip 10.00002pt\boldsymbol{\omega}_{t,s}=\mathbf{z}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t,s}.

(
\thechapter
.D.1)

In equation (
3.3
),
t_{1}\geq t_{2}+\lambda_{1}>t_{2}+1
for sufficiently large
T,
so

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}=\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}+\boldsymbol{\omega}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}},

and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}
is measurable in
\mathcal{F}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1},
which is independent of
\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\right]=\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}\right]\right)^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\right]\right)=0,

so

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\right]=\mathbf{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\omega}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\right],

and

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{t_{1}=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=(t_{1}-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t_{1}-\lambda_{1}}\left(\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\right]\right)\right\|_{M/2}\leq I+II,

where

\displaystyle I=\left\|\sum_{t_{1}=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=(t_{1}-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t_{1}-\lambda_{1}}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\right\|_{M/2},

and

\displaystyle II=\left\|\sum_{t_{1}=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=(t_{1}-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t_{1}-\lambda_{1}}\left(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\omega}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\right]\right)\right\|_{M/2}.

For
\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right),

\displaystyle I\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left\|\sum_{t_{1}=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=(t_{1}-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t_{1}-\lambda_{1}}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right)\right\|_{M/2}.

Notice that

\displaystyle\sum_{t_{1}=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=(t_{1}-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t_{1}-\lambda_{1}}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t_{2}=1}^{T-\lambda_{1}}\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right).

For any
j\geq 0
and
z=1,\cdots,T-\lambda_{1},
define

\displaystyle B_{z,j}=\sum_{t_{2}=T-\lambda_{1}-z+1}^{T-\lambda_{1}}\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right)

and
\mathcal{B}_{z,j}
the
\sigma
-field generated by
e_{T},e_{T-1},\cdots,e_{T-\lambda_{1}-z+1-j}.
Then
B_{z,j}
is measurable in
\mathcal{B}_{z,j},

\mathcal{B}_{z,j}\subset\mathcal{B}_{z+1,j},
and

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[B_{z+1,j}-B_{z,j}\mid\mathcal{B}_{z,j}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{t_{1}=T-\lambda_{1}-z+\lambda_{1}}^{(T-\lambda_{1}-z+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-T+\lambda_{1}+z-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}(T-\lambda_{1}-z)}\right.

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{T-\lambda_{1}-z}\mid\mathcal{F}_{T-\lambda_{1}-z,j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{T-\lambda_{1}-z}\mid\mathcal{F}_{T-\lambda_{1}-z,j-1}\right]\right)\mid\mathcal{B}_{z,j}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t_{1}=T-\lambda_{1}-z+\lambda_{1}}^{(T-\lambda_{1}-z+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-T+\lambda_{1}+z-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}(T-\lambda_{1}-z)}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{T-\lambda_{1}-z}\mid\mathcal{F}_{T-\lambda_{1}-z,j-1}\right]\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{T-\lambda_{1}-z}\mid\mathcal{F}_{T-\lambda_{1}-z,j-1}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=0.

Therefore,
B_{z,j}
forms a martingale, and from Theorem 1.1 of
Burkholder et al. 1972
,

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{t_{1}=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=(t_{1}-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t_{1}-\lambda_{1}}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right)\right\|_{M/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{\sum_{t_{2}=1}^{T-\lambda_{1}}\left\|\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right)\right\|^{2}_{M/2}}

\displaystyle=C\sqrt{\sum_{t_{2}=1}^{T-\lambda_{1}}\left\|\left(\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\right)\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right)\right\|^{2}_{M/2}}.

For any real-value vector
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbf{R}^{d},
since

\displaystyle\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}\right]\right)^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{T,T-t_{2}-1}\right]\right)^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{a},

from equation (
3.2
) in the manuscript,

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{a}\right\|_{M/2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathbf{E}\left[\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{a}\mid\mathcal{F}_{T,T-t_{2}-1}\right]\right\|_{M/2}

(
\thechapter
.D.2)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\mathbf{a}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}^{\top}\mathbf{z}_{t_{1}}\right\|_{M/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\left|\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{a}\right|_{2}^{2}}\leq C\left|\mathbf{a}\right|_{2}\sqrt{\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\left|\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\right|^{2}_{2}}.

For
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}
is measurable in the
\sigma
-field generated by
e_{t_{1}},\cdots,e_{t_{2}+1},

\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}
is independent of
\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right],
and from
Examples 1.22 of
Çınlar 2011
, for any vector
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbf{R}^{d},

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\left|\left(\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\right)\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right)\right|^{M/2}\right.

\displaystyle\mid\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]=\mathbf{a}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\left[\left|\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{a}\right|^{M/2}\right]\leq C\left(\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\left|\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\right|^{2}_{2}\right)^{M/4}\left|\mathbf{a}\right|_{2}^{M/2},

and

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\right)\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right)\right\|_{M/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\sqrt{\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\left|\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\right|^{2}_{2}}\ \left\|\ \left|\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right|_{2}\ \right\|_{M/2}.

Since

\displaystyle\left\|\ \left|\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right|_{2}\ \right\|_{M/2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\ \sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}^{(i)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}^{(i)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right)^{2}}\right\|_{M/2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}^{(i)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}^{(i)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right)^{2}\right\|_{M/4}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\|\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}^{(i)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}^{(i)}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right\|^{2}_{M/2}}\leq\sqrt{d}\delta_{j},

we have

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\right)\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right)\right\|_{M/2}

\displaystyle\leq C\delta_{j}\sqrt{d\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\left|\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\right|^{2}_{2}},

and

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{t_{1}=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=(t_{1}-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t_{1}-\lambda_{1}}\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{2},j-1}\right]\right)\right\|_{M/2}

(
\thechapter
.D.3)

\displaystyle\leq C\delta_{j}\sqrt{d\sum_{t_{2}=1}^{T-\lambda_{1}}\sum_{t_{1}=t_{2}+\lambda_{1}}^{(t_{2}+\lambda_{2})\wedge T}\left|\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\right|^{2}_{2}}=C\delta_{j}\sqrt{d\sum_{t_{1}=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=(t_{1}-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t_{1}-\lambda_{1}}\left|\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\right|^{2}_{2}}.

From (
\thechapter.D.3
),

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle\leq\left(C\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\delta_{j}\right)\sqrt{d\sum_{t_{1}=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=(t_{1}-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t_{1}-\lambda_{1}}\left|\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\right|^{2}_{2}}

(
\thechapter
.D.4)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\sqrt{d\sum_{t_{1}=1+\lambda_{1}}^{T}\sum_{t_{2}=(t_{1}-\lambda_{2})\vee 1}^{t_{1}-\lambda_{1}}\left|\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\right|^{2}_{2}}.

For the second part, define

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\omega}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{1},-1}\right]=\mathbf{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\omega}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\right],

then

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omega}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}-\mathbf{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\omega}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\omega}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{1},j}\right]-\mathbf{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\omega}_{t_{1},t_{1}-t_{2}-1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{t_{1}t_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{t_{2}}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t_{1},j-1}\right]\right),

which makes
